Infinitely many isolas of modulational instability for Stokes waves
Massimiliano Berti, Livia Corsi, Alberto Maspero, Paolo Ventura

Abstract




Abstract.

This paper proves long-standing conjectures regarding the
existence of
infinitely
many
high-frequency modulational instability
“isolas”
for a Stokes wave in
arbitrary depth
\mathtt{h}>0
,
under
longitudinal perturbations.
We provide a complete characterization of the unstable spectral bands in the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-spectrum of the water wave equations linearized around a Stokes wave of sufficiently small amplitude
\epsilon
.
The unstable spectrum
is the union of
isolated
“
isolas
” of elliptical shape,
indexed by integers
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
,
each with semiaxis of size

|\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})|\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}+2})
.
As first key achievement, we obtain
an explicit formula for the coefficient
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})

for
any

\mathtt{p}\geq 2
, that
remarkably depends
solely on
the maximal
Taylor-Fourier coefficients of the Stokes wave.
We provide simple expressions of the asymptotic expansion of such
coefficients in the shallow-water limit

\mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}
, for any
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
.
This allows to establish that the analytic function
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
is
not zero for any
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
,
by verifying that a combinatorial sum is not zero;
this relies on a crucial combinatorial identity due to Koutschan, van Hoeij,
and Zeilberger.

1
Introduction

Stokes waves are among the most renowned solutions of the gravity water waves equations. Discovered in the groundbreaking work

[
44
]
of Stokes in 1847,
these waves are spatially periodic and travel
steadly in the
x
-direction,
persisting for all times
despite dispersive effects.
The first mathematically rigorous proof of their existence was given by
[
45
,
33
,
41
]
almost one century ago.

A fundamental physical question concerns their stability or instability.
The mathematical problem can be formulated as follows. Consider
a
2\pi
-periodic Stokes wave
with amplitude
0<\epsilon\ll 1

in an ocean with depth
\mathtt{h}>0
.
The linearized water waves equations at the Stokes waves
are, in the reference frame moving with the speed of the wave,
a linear autonomous system

\partial_{t}h(t,x)=\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(\mathtt{h})\,[h(t,x)]

(1.1)

where

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(\mathtt{h})

is a real Hamiltonian pseudo-differential operator with
2\pi
-periodic coefficients, cfr. (
2.10
).
The dynamics
of (
1.1
) for
perturbations
h(t,\cdot)
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is fully determined by answering the following

•

Question:

What is the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
spectrum of

\mathcal{L}_{\epsilon}(\mathtt{h})
?

So far, it has only been known that such spectrum contains a figure “8” close to the origin
[
12
,
42
,
6
,
8
,
9
]
and an isola right above it

[
30
,
20
,
21
,
31
,
10
]

(and its symmetric one below).
Based on numerical simulations,
Deconink-Oliveras
[
23
]
conjectured
the existence of an infinite sequence
of isolated unstable spectral bands within the spectrum,
located along the imaginary axis,
exhibiting a near-elliptical shape –named “isolas”–
shrinking exponentially fast away from the origin.

The objective of this paper is to rigorously prove their existence.

Theorem 1.1
.

For any integer
{\mathtt{p}}\geq 2
, there exist
\epsilon_{1}^{({\mathtt{p}})}>0
and a closed set of isolated depths
{\mathcal{S}}^{({\mathtt{p}})}\subset(0,+\infty)
such that for any

\mathtt{h}\notin{\mathcal{S}}^{({\mathtt{p}})}
and
0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{1}^{({\mathtt{p}})}
, the spectrum
\sigma_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}(\mathtt{h})\big)
contains

\mathtt{p}-1
disjoint “isolas” in the complex upper half-plane,
see Figure
1
, plus the symmetric ones in
the lower half-plane.
For any

\ell=2,\dots,{\mathtt{p}}
, the
\ell
-th isola
is approximated by an ellipse
with semiaxes of size

\sim|\beta_{1}^{(\ell)}(\mathtt{h})|\epsilon^{\ell}
, centered
on the imaginary axis at a point

\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\ell)}(\mathtt{h})+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
,
where

0<\omega_{*}^{(2)}(\mathtt{h})<\dots<\omega_{*}^{(\ell)}(\mathtt{h})<\dots\qquad\text{satisfy}\qquad\lim_{\ell\to+\infty}\omega_{*}^{(\ell)}(\mathtt{h})=+\infty\,.

(1.2)

Each
\beta_{1}^{(\ell)}(\mathtt{h})
is a non-zero analytic function of the depth

\mathtt{h}>0
.

(a)

*

[.45]

(b)

*

[.45]

Figure 1
:

Spectral bands with non zero real part of the

L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-spectrum of

{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
.
On the right, zoom of the
\mathtt{p}
-th unstable isola.
Its center
y_{0}^{({\mathtt{p}})}(\epsilon)
is
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
distant from
\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{({\mathtt{p}})}
and its size is
\propto\epsilon^{{\mathtt{p}}}
. As shown in Theorem
1.2
, two unstable
eigenvalues
\lambda^{\pm}_{\mathtt{p}}(\mu,\epsilon)
of the Floquet operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
span the
\mathtt{p}
-th isola for Floquet exponents
\mu\in(\mu_{\wedge}^{({\mathtt{p}})}(\epsilon),\mu_{\vee}^{({\mathtt{p}})}(\epsilon))
and recollide on the imaginary axis at the ends of the interval.

A more complete statement is given in Theorem
1.2
.
We point out that
the number of isolas
in the spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}(\mathtt{h})

given by Theorem
1.1

is
arbitrarily large
,
provided
\epsilon
is sufficiently small.

Let us summarize the state of the art
on this problem.

In the sixties
Benjamin and Feir
[
2
,
1
]
, Whitham
[
48
]
, Lighthill
[
35
]
and Zakharov
[
51
,
54
]
discovered,
through experiments and formal arguments, that small amplitude Stokes waves in sufficiently deep water are modulationally unstable
when subject to long-wave perturbations.
This phenomenon, called “Benjamin-Feir” –or modulational– instability,
is nowadays supported by an enormous amount of
physical observations and numerical simulations, see e.g.
[
23
,
37
]
,
and detected in several dispersive and fluid PDE models, such as KdV, gKdV, NLS, Whitham equation, …, see
[
49
,
43
,
25
,
26
,
14
,
32
,
28
,
13
,
29
,
38
]
.

In mathematical terms, this phenomenon corresponds to the emergence of spectral bands
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
close to zero, spanned by
eigenvalues with non zero real part.
The first rigorous proof
of local branches of Benjamin-Feir spectrum was obtained by Bridges-Mielke
[
12
]
in finite depth, see also
[
30
]
, and by Nguyen-Strauss
[
42
]

in deep water. We also mention the nonlinear result in Chen-Su
[
15
]
. Recently
Berti-Maspero-Ventura
[
6
,
7
,
9
]
proved that these local branches extend to form a complete figure “8”,
as observed numerically in
[
23
]
.
This settles the question about the shape of the spectrum close to the origin.

Preliminary numerical studies concerning the existence of unstable spectrum situated away from the origin were carried out by McLean

[
39
,
40
]
in the 1980s, analyzing longitudinal and transversal wave perturbations. Later Deconinck and Oliveras
[
23
]

numerically
computed the first isolas of high-frequency instability
in the longitudinal case and
conjectured
the existence of infinitely many ones parameterized by integers
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
.
In the last years a lot of
work has been devoted to this problem yielding the
formulation of the following conjectures, see e.g.
[
17
,
18
]
:
for any
\mathtt{p}\geq 2

\displaystyle\bullet\textup{ \lx@text@underline{Weak conjecture}: for any depth $\mathtt{h}>0$, the size of the $\mathtt{p}$-th unstable isola, if present, is $\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}})$.}

(
W)

\displaystyle\bullet\textup{ \lx@text@underline{Strong conjecture}:
for ``many" depths, the $\mathtt{p}$-th isola
exists and with size asymptotically $\sim\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}}$. }

(
S)

Clearly (
S
) implies (
W
).
Rigorous results have been proved for
\mathtt{p}=2

in finite depth
in
[
30
]
and for deep water
in
[
10
]
.
For
\mathtt{p}=3
a formal result is given in
[
19
]
.

Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
solve both
(
W
) and (
S
) for any
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
, as well as the

\displaystyle\bullet

Improved weak conjecture
:
for the
“degenerate” depths
excluded in (
S
)
the
\mathtt{p}
-th unstable isola,

if present, has size
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}+2})
, for any

\mathtt{p}\geq 2
.

(
W’)

We also mention the results
[
20
,
21
,
31
,
27
]

regarding transversal wave perturbations.

A fundamental difficulty in proving (
S
) lies
in establishing the non-degeneracy of a
specific coefficient which characterizes
the real part of the eigenvalues.
When
\mathtt{p}=2

this coefficient depends
solely on
the second-order Taylor expansion of the Stokes wave
(on fourth-order in the degenerate case
[
10
]

and third-order for transversal perturbations in
[
20
,
21
]
)
thus enabling explicit computations
with a manageable effort
(frequently assisted by computer algebra systems and numerical methods).

On the other hand, the rigorous proof of
the strong conjecture (
S
)
faces the following
challenges:

(1)

Obtaining the expression of the relevant Taylor coefficient
–the function
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
in Theorem
1.1
–
for
any
integer
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
.
We achieve the result in Theorem
5.3
, that provides
an explicit formula of
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
in terms of the “maximal”
Taylor-Fourier coefficients of the Stokes wave.

(2)

Demonstrating that this
coefficient
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\not\equiv 0
does not vanish identically in
\mathtt{h}
for any
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
.
This is proved in Section
6

relying on the
formulas for
the maximal Taylor-Fourier
coefficients of the Stokes waves in the
shallow limit

\mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}
, given in
Proposition
3.14
.

Both steps are considerably demanding.
Although Kato’s perturbation theory of eigenvalues is algorithmic in principle, its direct practical implementation proves computationally infeasible.
Inputting all the
extensive power series coefficients of the Stokes waves
at any order in
\epsilon
,
along with
the Taylor expansion of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator, and calculating the numerous Kato projectors
required to
compute the expression of the function

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
for any
\mathtt{p}
,
is not practicable, even with current computing power. Furthermore, although recursive, the power series expansion of the Stokes wave coefficients,
are not explicitly known at any order,
which is
required to check the non vanishing of

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
for any
\mathtt{p}
.

In Section
1.1

we describe in detail the results
and, in Section
1.2
, the main ideas to achieve them.

1.1
Main result

Since the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}:={\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}(\mathtt{h})
has
2\pi
-periodic coefficients, the starting point to obtain Theorem
1.1
is the Bloch-Floquet decomposition of the spectrum

\sigma_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\big)=\bigcup_{\mu\in\big[-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\big)}\sigma_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}\big)\,\quad\textup{where}\quad{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}:={\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}(\mathtt{h}):=e^{-\mathrm{i}\,\mu x}\,{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\,\mu x}

(1.3)

is a complex pseudo-differential
operator,
with
2\pi
coefficients,
depending on
\mu
, see (
2.12
). The spectrum of

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}

on

L^{2}(\mathbb{T})

is discrete and its eigenvalues span, as
\mu
varies, the continuous spectral bands of
\sigma_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\big({\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}\big)
.
The parameter

\mu
is usually referred to as the Floquet exponent.
A direct consequence of the Bloch-Floquet decomposition is that any
solution
of (
1.1
) can be decomposed as a linear superposition of Bloch-waves

h(t,x)=e^{\lambda t}e^{\mathrm{i}\,\mu x}v(x)

(1.4)

where
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}

with associated eigenvector

v(x)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
.
If
\lambda
is unstable, i.e. has positive real part, then the
solution
(
1.4
)
grows exponentially fast in time.

We remark that the spectrum

\sigma_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon})

is a set which is

1
-periodic in
\mu
, and thus
it is sufficient to consider

\mu
in the first zone of Brillouin
[-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})
. Furthermore by the reality
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}
the spectrum
\sigma_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}({\mathcal{L}}_{-\mu,\epsilon})

is the complex conjugated of

\sigma_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon})

and we can restrict to
\mu\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2})
.

The Hamiltonian nature of the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
constrains the eigenvalues with nonzero real part to arise, for
small
\epsilon>0
, as perturbation of
multiple
eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}(\mathtt{h})
. The spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}(\mathtt{h})
is purely imaginary.
As stated in Lemma
2.9
, for any

\mathtt{h}>0
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}(\mathtt{h})
possesses, away from
0
, only simple or double eigenvalues:
there exists a diverging sequence of
Floquet exponents

\underline{\mu}=\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})>0\,,\qquad\mathtt{p}\in\mathbb{N}\,,\ \mathtt{p}\geq 2\,,

(and its integer shifts
\underline{\mu}+k
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
), such that

{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{\mu},0}(\mathtt{h})
possesses double eigenvalues

\{\pm\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\}_{\mathtt{p}=2,3,\dots}

forming a diverging sequence
as in (
1.2
).
In other words the unperturbed spectral bands of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}(\mathtt{h})
intersect at
\underline{\mu}=\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})
,
mod
1
.

To state the next result, we introduce the following notation.

•

Notation
.
We denote by
\mathcal{O}(y^{m_{1}}\epsilon^{n_{1}},\dots,y^{m_{q}}\epsilon^{n_{q}})
(
y=\mu
,
\delta
or
\nu
depending on the context)
an analytic function
with values in a Banach space
X
that satisfies, for some
C>0
and
(y,\epsilon)
small, the bound

\|\mathcal{O}(y^{m_{1}}\epsilon^{n_{1}},\dots,y^{m_{q}}\epsilon^{n_{q}})\|_{X}\leq C\sum_{j=1}^{q}|y|^{m_{j}}|\epsilon|^{n_{j}}\,.

We denote by
r(y^{m_{1}}\epsilon^{n_{1}},\dots,y^{m_{q}}\epsilon^{n_{q}})
any
scalar function
\mathcal{O}(y^{m_{1}}\epsilon^{n_{1}},\dots,y^{m_{q}}\epsilon^{n_{q}})
which is also real analytic. For any
\delta>0
, we denote by
B_{\delta}(x)
the real interval
(x-\delta,x+\delta)
centered at
x
.

The next result sharply describes
the spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}(\mathtt{h})

near
\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})

for any
(\mu,\epsilon)
close to
(\underline{\mu},0)
.

Theorem 1.2
.

(Spectral Bloch-Floquet
bands)
For any integer
\mathtt{p}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
, for any
\mathtt{h}>0
let
\underline{\mu}=\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})>0
(given by Lemma
2.7
) such that the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{\mu},0}(\mathtt{h})
in (
2.17
) has a
double eigenvalue at
\mathrm{i}\,\omega^{(\mathtt{p})}_{*}(\mathtt{h})
.
Then there exist

i)

a non zero real analytic function

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
defined for any

\mathtt{h}>0
, satisfying

\lim_{\mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}}\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})=-\infty\,,\qquad\quad\lim_{\mathtt{h}\to+\infty}\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})=0\,;

(1.5)

ii)

constants

\epsilon_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})},\delta_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}>0
and real analytic functions
\mu_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})},\mu_{\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})},\mu_{\wedge}^{(\mathtt{p})}\colon[0,\epsilon_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})})\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\mu_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}(0)=\mu_{\wedge}^{(\mathtt{p})}(0)=\mu_{\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})}(0)=\underline{\mu}

and, for any

0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}
,

\mu_{\wedge}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)<\mu_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)<\mu_{\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)\,,\quad|\mu_{\wedge,\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)-\mu_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)|=\frac{2|\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})|}{T_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})}\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}}+r(\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}+{2}})\,,

(1.6)

(see Figure
2
) where
T_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})>0

is an
analytic function for any

\mathtt{h}>0
;

such that

•

(Unstable eigenvalues)

for any
(\mu,\epsilon)\in B_{\delta_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}}(\underline{\mu})\times B_{\epsilon_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}}(0)

the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}(\mathtt{h})
possesses a pair of eigenvalues

\displaystyle\lambda^{\pm}_{\mathtt{p}}\big(\mu,\epsilon\big)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}+\mathrm{i}\,s^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)\pm\dfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{D^{(\mathtt{p})}\big(\mu,\epsilon\big)}&\mbox{if }\mu\in\big(\mu_{\wedge}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon),\mu_{\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)\big)\,,\\[8.53581pt]
\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}+\mathrm{i}\,s^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)\pm\mathrm{i}\,\sqrt{\big|D^{(\mathtt{p})}\big(\mu,\epsilon\big)\big|}&\mbox{if }\mu\notin\big(\mu_{\wedge}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon),\mu_{\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)\big)\,,\end{cases}

(1.7)

where
s^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)
,
D^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)
are real-analytic functions of the form

\displaystyle s^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)=r(\epsilon^{2},\nu)\qquad\text{where}\qquad\nu:=\mu-\mu_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)\,,

(1.8)

\displaystyle D^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)=4(\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h}))^{2}\epsilon^{2\mathtt{p}}-(T_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h}))^{2}\nu^{2}+r(\epsilon^{2\mathtt{p}+{2}},\nu\epsilon^{2\mathtt{p}},\nu^{2}\epsilon^{{2}},\nu^{3})\,.

For any
\mathtt{h}>0
such that

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\neq 0
the function

D^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)\ \text{is}\ \begin{cases}>0\qquad\ \forall\mu\in\big(\mu_{\wedge}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon),\mu_{\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)\big)\,\\
=0\qquad\ \forall\mu\in\{\mu_{\wedge}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)\,,\mu_{\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)\}\\
<0\qquad\ \forall\mu\notin\big(\mu_{\wedge}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon),\mu_{\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)\big)\,.\end{cases}

•

(Unstable isola)
If
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\neq 0
then, for any fixed
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})})
, as
\mu
varies in
\big(\mu_{\wedge}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon),\mu_{\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)\big)

the pair of unstable eigenvalues
\lambda^{\pm}_{\mathtt{p}}(\mu,\epsilon)
in (
1.7
) describes
a closed analytic curve
in the complex plane

\lambda=x+\mathrm{i}\,y

that
intersects orthogonally the imaginary axis,
encircles a convex region, and it is symmetric
with respect to
y
-axis.
Such isola is
approximated by an ellipse

x^{2}+(E^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h}))^{2}(1+r(\epsilon^{2}))\Big(y-y_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)\Big)^{2}=(\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h}))^{2}\epsilon^{2\mathtt{p}}(1+r(\epsilon^{{2}}))

(1.9)

where
E^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\in(0,1)
is
a real analytic function for any

\mathtt{h}>0

and
y_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)

is
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
-close to
\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
.

•

(Degenerate depths)
If
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})=0
the real part of the unstable eigenvalues
\lambda^{\pm}_{\mathtt{p}}(\mu,\epsilon)
, if present, is of size
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}+2})
.

Theorem

1.2
follows by
Theorem

2.11
,
the abstract Theorem
4.7

and Lemma
4.8
.

Let us make some comments.

Figure 2
:
The instability region around the curve
\mu_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)
delimited by the curves

\mu_{\wedge}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)
and
\mu_{\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)
.

1.

Weak conjecture, upper bounds:

Theorem

1.2
proves the weak conjecture (
W
) for all depths.
Indeed, in view of (
1.8
) and (
1.6
), for any
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
and any depth
\mathtt{h}>0
,
the real part of the eigenvalues
(
1.7
)
is at most of size
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}})

implying that the unstable isolas,
if ever exist, shrink exponentially fast as
\mathtt{p}\to+\infty
.

2.

Strong conjecture,
lower bounds:

Theorem

1.2
proves the strong conjecture (
S
) for all depths such that
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\neq 0
.
In this case
the real part of the eigenvalues
(
1.7
)
is of size
|\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})|\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}+2})

and by (
1.9
)
the
\mathtt{p}
-th unstable isola has
elliptical shape.
The most unstable eigenvalue is reached at
\nu\propto\!\epsilon^{2\mathtt{p}}
, see (
4.36
).
Note also
that by (
1.9
) each isola drifts
of
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
from its known zeroth-order center

\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
, thus rather quickly relative to its
\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}}
-size.

3.

Unstable Floquet exponents:

By (
1.6
), the portion of the
unstable spectral
\mathtt{p}
-th isola
is parametrized by Floquet exponents
\mu

in the very narrow interval

\big(\mu_{\wedge}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon),\mu_{\vee}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\epsilon)\big)
which has exponentially small
width

\sim\frac{4|\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})|}{T_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})}\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}}
.

4.

Non degeneracy of the function
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
:

we
analytically
prove

that, for
any

\mathtt{p}\geq 2
, the map

\mathtt{h}\mapsto\beta^{(\mathtt{p})}_{1}(\mathtt{h})
is real analytic and
satisfies (
1.5
).
In the next section we explain how we achieve this challenging result.
For
\mathtt{p}=2,3,4
the graphs of

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
are numerically plotted in Figures

3
.

Figure 3
:
Cases
\mathtt{p}=2,3,4
.
The function
\beta_{1}^{(2)}(\mathtt{h})
vanishes
only at the red point

\mathtt{h}_{*}^{(2)}\approx 1.84940
coherently with
[
19
,
30
]
. The function
\beta_{1}^{(3)}(\mathtt{h})
vanishes only at the red point
\mathtt{h}_{*}^{(3)}\approx 0.82064
coherently with
[
19
]
. the function
\beta_{1}^{(4)}(\mathtt{h})
vanishes only at the red points
\mathtt{h}_{*}^{(4)}\approx 0.566633
and
\mathtt{h}_{**}^{(4)}\approx 1.255969
.

5.

Improved weak conjecture:

Each function
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
vanishes only at a closed set of isolated depths.
The set
{\mathcal{S}}^{(\mathtt{p})}

in Theorem
1.1
is the union

{\mathcal{S}}^{(\mathtt{p})}:=\bigcup_{\ell=2}^{\mathtt{p}}\big(\beta_{1}^{(\ell)}\big)^{-1}(0)\,.

When
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})=0

it is necessary to further expand the discriminant
D^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)

in (
1.8
)
to determine its sign. The latter degeneracy occurs
at

\mathtt{h}=+\infty
for
every

\mathtt{p}\geq 2
since
\lim_{\mathtt{h}\to+\infty}\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})=0
,
as we prove in Proposition
6.7
.
Such degeneration ultimately descends from a
structural
property of the Stokes waves in deep water at any order proved in Proposition

3.16
.

The finding looks as a further interesting aspect of the integrability properties of the deep water wave equations, discussed in
[
53
,
3
,
50
,
22
]
.

The last item of Theorem
1.2

actually
proves the
improved weak conjecture:
if

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})=0

(degenerate depths),
the
\mathtt{p}
-th unstable isola, if present, has size
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}+2})
,
for any

\mathtt{p}\geq 2
.

For
\mathtt{p}=2
the
computations required to detect the unstable isola of size
\sim\epsilon^{4}
at the degenerate depth

\mathtt{h}=+\infty

have been performed
in
[
10
]
.

6.

Conjectures:
In light of Theorem
1.2
we conjecture that

(a)

The analytic function
\mathtt{h}\mapsto\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})

has only a finite number of roots for any
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
.

(b)

For any
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
the
\mathtt{p}
-th isola exists also for degenerate depths and
its size is
\sim\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}+2}
.

The following section comments on the main difficulties and key
novelties of the proof.

1.2
Ideas of proof

The overall goal is to compute the eigenvalues of the Hamiltonian and reversible
operator

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}=\begin{bmatrix}(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)\circ({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))&|D+\mu|\tanh\big((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D+\mu|\big)\\
-(1+a_{\epsilon}(x))&({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)\end{bmatrix}

(1.10)

that branch off from the
\mathtt{p}
-th double eigenvalue
\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{\mu},0}(\mathtt{h})
for
(\mu,\epsilon)
close to
(\underline{\mu},0)
. The functions
a_{\epsilon}(x)
,
p_{\epsilon}(x)
are real-analytic
2\pi
-periodic even functions that depend on the Stokes wave
(\eta_{\epsilon}(x),\psi_{\epsilon}(x))
along which we linearize the water waves equations (
2.1
). In (
1.10
) the constant
{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}=\sqrt{\tanh(\mathtt{h})}
is the linear speed of the Stokes wave.

In Section
4
,
following the general approach in
[
6
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
, we reduce the spectral problem to study the eigenvalues of a

2\times 2
symplectic and reversible matrix, i.e. of the form (cfr. (
4.7
))

\mathtt{L}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)=\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\,\alpha^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)&\beta^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)\\
\beta^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)&\mathrm{i}\,\gamma^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)\end{pmatrix}\,

(1.11)

where
\alpha^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)
,
\gamma^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)

and
\beta^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)

are real analytic functions depending on
the Stokes wave.
In particular the function

\beta^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)
is given by the scalar product

\beta^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mu,\epsilon)=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\,}(\mathfrak{B}(\mu,\epsilon)f_{0}^{-},f_{\mathtt{p}}^{+})

(1.12)

where

\mathfrak{B}(\mu,\epsilon)

is the operator (
4.9
)
obtained by the Kato similarity reduction
process and

f_{0}^{-}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\Omega(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}),\mathtt{h})}}\begin{bmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\,\,\Omega(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}),\mathtt{h})\\
1\end{bmatrix}\,,\ f_{\mathtt{p}}^{+}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\Omega(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})+\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})}}\begin{bmatrix}-\Omega(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})+\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})\\
\mathrm{i}\,\end{bmatrix}e^{\mathrm{i}\,\mathtt{p}x}\,,

(1.13)

are the unperturbed eigenvectors
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{\mu},0}
in (
2.32
) associated to the double eigenvalue
\mathrm{i}\,\omega^{(\mathtt{p})}_{*}(\mathtt{h})
.
Note that
\mathtt{p}
is the “spectral gap” between the
harmonics of the eigenvectors

f_{0}^{-}
and
f_{\mathtt{p}}^{+}
in (
1.13
).

The crucial step in proving Theorem
1.2
is to show that

\beta^{(\mathtt{p})}(\underline{\mu}+\delta,\epsilon)=\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}}+\text{h.o.t}\qquad\text{at}\qquad\underline{\mu}=\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})\,,

(1.14)

and prove that

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})

is not zero.
We now list
various obstacles encountered, as discussed in items (1)
and (2) on page
1
, and the corresponding
ideas for addressing them.

(1) The exact expression of the function
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
.
In view of (
1.12
),
the first step to prove (
1.14
) is to compute the Taylor expansion of the operator
\mathfrak{B}(\mu,\epsilon)
at
(\underline{\mu},0)
up to order
\mathtt{p}
.
This is achieved in Proposition
5.8
, which provides
an explicit formula for its Taylor coefficients
involving those of the operator
{\mathcal{B}}(\mu,\epsilon)
defined in (
2.12
)
and of the spectral projector

P(\mu,\epsilon)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\,}\oint_{\Gamma}({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}-\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\lambda
.
This expression depends on all Taylor coefficients of the Stokes wave expansion up to order
\mathtt{p}
. Computing
all such coefficients at an arbitrary order
\mathtt{p}
is prohibitively complex and lengthy, even with computer-assistance.
However, we prove this is unnecessary:

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})

depends solely on a select subset of
Taylor-Fourier coefficients that we identify,
drastically reducing computational complexity.

To prove this, our new strategy fully leverages a structural property of the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
that propagates throughout Kato’s reduction scheme.
This property, put simply, is that the matrix representing the action of each “jet”

{\mathcal{L}}_{i,n}:=\frac{\delta^{i}\epsilon^{j}}{i!n!}(\partial_{\mu}^{i}\partial_{\epsilon}^{n}{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{\mu},0})

of the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
exhibits
only “finitely many bands”:
precisely, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, for any
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}

({\mathcal{L}}_{i,n}\,v_{1}e^{\mathrm{i}\,j_{1}x},\ v_{2}e^{\mathrm{i}\,j_{2}x})=0\ \ \mbox{ if }|j_{1}-j_{2}|>n\mbox{ or }j_{1}-j_{2}\not\equiv n\mbox{ mod }2\ .

(1.15)

We say that an operator of this form belongs to the class
\mathfrak{F}_{n}
, and a sum of
\ell
-homogeneous terms as

\sum_{i+n=\ell}{\mathcal{L}}_{i,n}
to the class
\mathtt{F}_{\ell}
, see Definition
5.4
.
For
A\in\mathfrak{F}_{n}
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}
,
its
\kappa
-th“band” operator
A^{[\kappa]}

is defined as the operator
with matrix coefficients
A^{j_{1}}_{j_{2}}

supported on the band

j_{2}-j_{1}=\kappa
only, see definitions (
5.2
) and (
5.3
).
A crucial fact is that the graded vector space
\bigoplus_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\mathtt{F}_{\ell}
is closed under composition,
if
A\in\mathtt{F}_{\ell}
and
B\in\mathtt{F}_{\ell^{\prime}}
then
A\circ B\in\mathtt{F}_{\ell+\ell^{\prime}}
.
This allows us to propagate this structure along Kato’s reduction scheme, and prove that the jets

\mathfrak{B}_{i,n}
of the operator
\mathfrak{B}(\mu,\epsilon)
belong to
\mathfrak{F}_{n}
.

Let us explain how this structure originates.
The crucial step is to observe that the Stokes wave has itself a very particular structure.
Precisely, expanding the Stokes waves in power series of
\epsilon
as

\eta_{\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon\cos(x)+\sum_{\ell\geq 2}\epsilon^{\ell}\eta_{\ell}(x)\,,\quad\psi_{\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{-1}\sin(x)+\sum_{\ell\geq 2}\epsilon^{\ell}\psi_{\ell}(x)\,

we prove in Theorem
2.3

(see also Section
3
),
that,
at
any
order
\ell
, the functions
\eta_{\ell}(x)
and
\psi_{\ell}(x)
are given by

\eta_{\ell}(x)=\sum_{\kappa=0\atop\kappa\equiv\ell\,\textup{mod}\,2}^{\ell}\eta_{\ell}^{[\kappa]}\cos(\kappa x)\,,\quad\psi_{\ell}(x)=\sum_{\kappa=1\atop\kappa\equiv\ell\,\textup{mod}\,2}^{\ell}\psi_{\ell}^{[\kappa]}\sin(\kappa x)\,,

(1.16)

with
harmonics
\kappa

not larger than
\ell
, and
with the same parity of
\ell
.
Consequently, the functions
a_{\epsilon}(x)
,
p_{\epsilon}(x)
, entering in the definition (
1.10
) of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
expand as

a_{\epsilon}(x)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\epsilon^{\ell}a_{\ell}(x)
,
p_{\epsilon}(x)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\epsilon^{\ell}p_{\ell}(x)
, where
each
a_{\ell}(x),p_{\ell}(x)
has
the form (
1.16
) as well.
This structure in turn implies
(
1.15
).

The fact that the jets
\mathfrak{B}_{i,n}
belong to
\mathfrak{F}_{n}
, for any
i
, i.e. fulfill (
1.15
),
implies immediately the expansion (
1.14
), since

(\mathfrak{B}_{i,n}f_{0}^{-},f_{\mathtt{p}}^{+})=0

whenever

n<\mathtt{p}
.
In Section
5.3
, this property is
exploited
to establish
an upper bound
of
\lesssim\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}}

for the real part of the eigenvalues on the unstable
spectral bands
(if they exist).
Summarizing

•

the “weak conjecture”
(
W
), as well as the
“improved weak conjecture”
(
W’
),
is proved by the algebraic properties of the spaces
\mathfrak{F}_{n}
, inherited by properties of the Stokes wave.

In order to prove also the strong conjecture (
S
)
we need an explicit expression of
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
.
Property (
1.15
) plays a crucial role also in
proving that
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
depends only on the
maximal
Fourier-Taylor coefficients
a_{\ell}^{[\ell]},p_{\ell}^{[\ell]}
with
|\ell|\leq\mathtt{p}
,
see Theorem
5.3
. Let us explain why with an example: a term of order

\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}}
in the Taylor expansion in (
1.12
) has the form

(A_{0,n_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ A_{0,n_{q}}f_{0}^{-},f_{\mathtt{p}}^{+})\quad\mbox{ with }n_{1}+\ldots+n_{q}=\mathtt{p}\ \textup{and }\ f_{0}^{-}\,,\;f_{\mathtt{p}}^{+}\textup{ in }\eqref{autovettorikernel0}\,,

(1.17)

for some operators

A_{0,n_{a}}\in\mathfrak{F}_{n_{a}}
.
In the scalar product (
1.17
)
only the band
(A_{0,n_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ A_{0,n_{q}})^{[\mathtt{p}]}
is relevant.
But since each
A_{0,n_{a}}
has bands
|\kappa_{a}|\leq n_{a}
, the only possibility to reach band
\mathtt{p}
is to take the highest band of each operator, namely

(A_{0,n_{1}}\circ\ldots\circ A_{0,n_{q}}f_{0}^{-},f_{\mathtt{p}}^{+})=(A_{0,n_{1}}^{[n_{1}]}\circ\ldots\circ A_{0,n_{q}}^{[n_{q}]}f_{0}^{-},f_{\mathtt{p}}^{+})\,.

This principle is used in Lemma
5.15
.
Furthermore, by the properties (
1.16
) of the Stokes wave, each
A_{0,n}^{[n]}
depends only on the highest maximal Fourier-Taylor coefficients
a_{n}^{[n]},p_{n}^{[n]}
.
Summarizing

•

the fact that the expansions
(
5.11
)-(
5.12
) of
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
depend of only the maximal Taylor-Fourier coefficients
a_{\ell}^{[\ell]},p_{\ell}^{[\ell]}
for

|\ell|\leq\mathtt{p}
of the Stokes waves, deeply exploits this “maximal harmonics” principle of the spaces
\mathfrak{F}_{n}
.

(2) Proof that
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\neq 0
for any
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
.

The function

\mathtt{h}\mapsto\beta^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
is analytic by direct inspection
of the formulas
(
5.11
)-(
5.12
)
since each
a_{\ell}^{[\ell]}(\mathtt{h}),p_{\ell}^{[\ell]}(\mathtt{h})
are analytic in
\mathtt{h}
, see Lemma
2.5
.
Such formulas
remain quite complex, involving
3^{\mathtt{p}-1}
terms.
Furthermore, explicit expressions for the
Taylor-Fourier coefficients
\eta_{\ell}^{[\ell]}(\mathtt{h})
and
\psi_{\ell}^{[\ell]}(\mathtt{h})

of the Stokes waves are unknown for any

\ell
, and consequently, the same holds for

a_{\ell}^{[\ell]}(\mathtt{h})
and

p_{\ell}^{[\ell]}(\mathtt{h})
.
Due to analyticity, proving Theorems

1.1
-
1.2
clearly
reduce to show that

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\neq 0
at some depth.
The terms in the sum for

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
could cancel each other out, resulting in an identically zero function.
Actually we demonstrate in Section
6.2

that this occurs, for
any

\mathtt{p}\geq 2
,
in the deep water limit
\mathtt{h}=+\infty
.

Thus we focus on the shallow water limit
\mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}
.
Surprisingly
in Section
3.2

we find that

•

the limits
of the maximal
Fourier-Taylor coefficients

\eta_{\ell}^{[\ell]}(\mathtt{h})
,

\psi_{\ell}^{[\ell]}(\mathtt{h})
of the Stokes wave as
\mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}
can be computed using an inductive procedure, based on the iterative bifurcation structure of the Stokes wave, resulting in hypergeometric quantities,
see (
3.26
), (
3.27
).

Thanks to these limits we deduce the
Laurent asymptotic expansion for
any

\ell
of

\displaystyle p_{\ell}^{[\ell]}(\mathtt{h})=-2\ell\Big(\frac{3}{8}\Big)^{\ell-1}\mathtt{h}^{3-3\ell-\frac{1}{2}}-\ell\Big(\frac{3}{8}\Big)^{\ell-1}\,\frac{11\ell^{2}-9\ell+1}{9}\mathtt{h}^{5-3\ell-\frac{1}{2}}+O(\mathtt{h}^{7-3\ell-\frac{1}{2}})\,,

(1.18)

\displaystyle a_{\ell}^{[\ell]}(\mathtt{h})=-\ell\Big(\frac{3}{8}\Big)^{\ell-1}\mathtt{h}^{2-3\ell}-\ell\Big(\frac{3}{8}\Big)^{\ell-1}\,\frac{31\ell^{2}-9\ell+2}{18}\mathtt{h}^{4-3\ell}+O(\mathtt{h}^{6-3\ell})\,.

In the shallow water limit the formula
(
5.11
)-(
5.12
)
for
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})

has remarkable simplifications since
many addends are negligible, see Lemma

6.3
,
and, using (
1.18
), we finally deduce
in Section
6.1
,
the
Laurent
asymptotic expansion
of

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
as
\mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}
,

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})=\underbrace{{\widetilde{A}}^{(\mathtt{p})}}_{=0}\mathtt{h}^{\tfrac{7}{2}-3\mathtt{p}}+\underbrace{{\widetilde{C}}^{(\mathtt{p})}}_{<0}\mathtt{h}^{\tfrac{11}{2}-3\mathtt{p}}+O(\mathtt{h}^{\tfrac{15}{2}-3\mathtt{p}})\quad\text{as}\quad\mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}\,.

(1.19)

As we prove in
Lemma
6.5

•

the first order term

\widetilde{A}^{(\mathtt{p})}
of such expansion
vanishes

for every
\mathtt{p}
, see
Appendix
B
.

That is why we compute
explicitly also the second order Laurent expansion term in (
1.19
).
In this way, the initial problem
of proving the
non-degeneracy of the analytic functions

\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
for any
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
,
has been reduced to determining
whether
{\widetilde{C}}^{(\mathtt{p})}
,
which is proportional to the
combinatorial sum (
6.11
), in non-zero. In conclusion

•

the first terms of

\widetilde{C}^{(\mathtt{p})}
are easily proved to be proportional
to
\frac{\mathtt{p}(\mathtt{p}+1)^{2}}{3}
.
Computer assisted computations
confirm this calculus up to, say, the first

\mathtt{p}\leq 20
,
suggesting that formula (
6.34
)
actually holds for any
\mathtt{p}
.
This is proved rigorously by the computer algebra methods in Zeilberger-van Hoeij-Koutschan
[
46
]
.
We provide in
[
11
]

a self-contained exposition of this approach.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2

we first present the water waves equations and in
Theorem
2.3
the structural properties
of the Taylor-Fourier
expansion of the Stokes waves at
any
order. Then we state
Theorem
2.11

that implies, with the results of Section
4
,
Theorems

1.1

and

1.2
.
In Section
3

we prove
Theorem
2.3
,
and the asymptotic limits of
the Stokes waves
as
\mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}
and

\mathtt{h}\to+\infty
.
Section
4
describes
the
splitting of the eigenvalues of

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
.
In Section

5
we find the expression of the function
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
in terms of the Taylor-Fourier
Stokes waves coefficients
and we prove the weak
conjecture (
W
).
Finally in Section
6
we
show the limits (
1.5
)
and prove the strong conjecture (
S
).

Notation
.
The symbol

a_{\epsilon}\sim b_{\epsilon}

means that

a_{\epsilon}b_{\epsilon}^{-1}\to 1
as

\epsilon\to 0
, whereas

a_{\epsilon}\propto b_{\epsilon}
means that
a_{\epsilon}\sim Cb_{\epsilon}
for some constant

C
.

Acknowledgments.

We thank W. Strauss, B. Deconinck and V. Hur for several insightful conversations about modulational instability. We thank D. Zeilberger, M. van Hoeij and C. Koutschan for the combinatorics of Lemma
6.6
.

2
High frequency instabilities of Stokes wave

We consider the Euler equations for a 2-dimensional
incompressible
and irrotational fluid under the action of gravity which fills the time dependent region

{\mathcal{D}}_{\eta}:=\big\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;-\mathtt{h}<y<\eta(t,x)\big\}\,,\quad\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\,,

with depth
\mathtt{h}>0

and space periodic boundary conditions.
The irrotational velocity field is the gradient
of the scalar potential
\Phi(t,x,y)

determined
as the unique harmonic solution of

\Delta\Phi=0\ \text{ in }\,{\mathcal{D}}_{\eta}\,,\quad\Phi(t,x,\eta(t,x))=\psi(t,x)\,,\quad\Phi_{y}(t,x,-\mathtt{h})=0\,.

The time evolution of the fluid is determined by two boundary conditions at the free surface.
The first is that the fluid particles remain, along the evolution, on the free surface
(kinematic boundary condition),
and the second one is that the pressure of the fluid
is equal, at the free surface, to the constant atmospheric pressure
(dynamic boundary condition).
Then, as shown by Zakharov
[
52
]
and Craig-Sulem
[
16
]
,
the time evolution of the fluid is determined by the
following equations for the unknowns
(\eta(t,x),\psi(t,x))
,

\eta_{t}=G(\eta)\psi\,,\quad\psi_{t}=-g\eta-\dfrac{\psi_{x}^{2}}{2}+\dfrac{1}{2(1+\eta_{x}^{2})}\big(G(\eta)\psi+\eta_{x}\psi_{x}\big)^{2}\,,

(2.1)

where
g>0
is the gravity constant and
G(\eta):=G(\eta,\mathtt{h})
denotes
the Dirichlet-Neumann operator

[G(\eta)\psi](x):=\Phi_{y}(x,\eta(x))-\Phi_{x}(x,\eta(x))\eta_{x}(x)\,.

With no loss of generality we set the gravity constant
g=1
.
The equations (
2.1
) are the Hamiltonian system

\partial_{t}\begin{bmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{bmatrix}=\mathcal{J}\begin{bmatrix}\nabla_{\eta}\mathscr{H}\\
\nabla_{\psi}\mathscr{H}\end{bmatrix},\quad\quad\mathcal{J}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathrm{Id}\\
-\mathrm{Id}&0\end{bmatrix},

where
\nabla
denote the
L^{2}
-gradient, and the Hamiltonian

\mathscr{H}(\eta,\psi):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\psi\,G(\eta)\psi+\eta^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x

is the sum of the kinetic and potential energy of the fluid.

In addition of being Hamiltonian, the water waves
system (
2.1
) is time reversible with respect to the involution

\rho\begin{bmatrix}\eta(x)\\
\psi(x)\end{bmatrix}:=\begin{bmatrix}\eta(-x)\\
-\psi(-x)\end{bmatrix},\quad\text{i.e. }\mathscr{H}\circ\rho=\mathscr{H}\,,

(2.2)

and it is invariant under space translations.

Stokes waves.

The nonlinear water waves equations (
2.1
)
admit periodic traveling solutions –called Stokes waves–

\eta(t,x)=\breve{\eta}(x-ct)\,,\quad\psi(t,x)=\breve{\psi}(x-ct)\,,

for some speed
c
(which depends on the amplitude of the wave) and
2\pi
-periodic profiles
\breve{\eta}(x),\breve{\psi}(x)
.
Bifurcation of small amplitude Stokes waves
has been rigorously proved by Levi-Civita
[
33
]
and
Nekrasov
[
41
]
in infinite depth,
and Struik
[
45
]
for finite depth.
Currently, this can be derived through a direct application
of the Crandall-Rabinowitz bifurcation theorem from a simple eigenvalue as in
[
7
]
.
The speed of Stokes waves of small amplitude is close to the one predicted by the linear theory,

{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}:=\sqrt{\tanh(\mathtt{h})}\,.

To establish the existence of all instability isolas of Stokes waves, we require structural insights into their Taylor expansion at every order. The knowledge of merely a finite number of Taylor coefficients is clearly insufficient for this purpose.
We introduce the following definitions.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote by
\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}

the vector space of real trigonometric polynomials of the form

f(x)=\begin{cases}f^{[0]}+f^{[2]}\cos(2x)+\dots+f^{[\ell]}\cos(\ell\,x)&\text{if }\ell\text{ is even}\,,\\
f^{[1]}\cos(x)+f^{[3]}\cos(3x)+\dots+f^{[\ell]}\cos(\ell\,x)&\text{if }\ell\text{ is odd}\,,\end{cases}

(2.3)

with real coefficients
f^{[i]}
,
i=0,\ldots,\ell
and by

\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}
the vector space of real trigonometric polynomials of the form

g(x)=\begin{cases}g^{[2]}\sin(2x)+g^{[4]}\sin(4x)+\dots+g^{[\ell]}\sin(\ell\,x)&\text{if }\ell\text{ is even}\,,\\
g^{[1]}\sin(x)+g^{[3]}\sin(3x)+\dots+g^{[\ell]}\sin(\ell\,x)&\text{if }\ell\text{ is odd}\,,\end{cases}

(2.4)

with real coefficients
g^{[i]}
,
i=1,\ldots,\ell
.

Note that a function
f\in\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}
, resp.
\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}
,
is not just an even, resp. odd,
trigonometric real polynomial of degree less or equal to
\ell
,
but it possesses only harmonics of the
same
parity of
\ell
.

The coefficients of the Stokes wave are found to be analytic functions of the depth
\mathtt{h}>0
, exhibiting a structure captured by the following definition.

Definition 2.2
.

An analytic function
g:(0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
belongs to

{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})
if there exist polynomials
p(x)

and
q(x)
with
integer
coefficients and without common factors, such that
q(x)\neq 0
for any
0<x\leq 1
, and

g(\mathtt{h})=\frac{p({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})}{q({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})}\,,\ \forall\mathtt{h}>0\,,\qquad{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}=\tanh(\mathtt{h})\,.

(2.5)

Note that
\lim\limits_{\mathtt{h}\to+\infty}g(\mathtt{h})=\frac{p(1)}{q(1)}\in\mathbb{Q}
. The
set
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})
is closed under the product of functions.

The subsequent result provides a detailed description
of the Stokes waves at
any
order in
\epsilon
.

Theorem 2.3
.

(Stokes waves)

For any
\mathtt{h}_{*}>0
there exist
\epsilon_{*}:=\epsilon_{*}(\mathtt{h}_{*})>0
and for any
\mathtt{h}\geq\mathtt{h}_{*}

a unique family of real analytic solutions
(\eta_{\epsilon}(x),\psi_{\epsilon}(x),c_{\epsilon})
,
defined for any
|\epsilon|<\epsilon_{*}
, of

\begin{cases}c\,\eta_{x}+G(\eta,\mathtt{h})\psi=0\,,\\
c\,\psi_{x}-\eta-\dfrac{\psi_{x}^{2}}{2}+\dfrac{1}{2(1+\eta_{x}^{2})}\big(G(\eta,\mathtt{h})\psi+\eta_{x}\psi_{x}\big)^{2}=0\,,\end{cases}

(2.6)

such that

\eta_{\epsilon}(x)
and
\psi_{\epsilon}(x)
are
2\pi
-periodic,
\eta_{\epsilon}(x)
is even and
\psi_{\epsilon}(x)
is odd, of the form

\displaystyle\eta_{\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon\cos(x)+{\mathop{\sum}}_{\ell\geq 2}\epsilon^{\ell}\eta_{\ell}(x)\,,

(2.7)

\displaystyle\psi_{\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{-1}\sin(x)+{\mathop{\sum}}_{\ell\geq 2}\epsilon^{\ell}\psi_{\ell}(x)\,,

\displaystyle c_{\epsilon}={\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+{\mathop{\sum}}_{\ell\geq 2,\ell\textup{ even}}\!\!\!\!\epsilon^{\ell}c_{\ell}\quad\text{with}\ {\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\ \text{defined in}\ \eqref{rationalfunctions}\,.

The functions

\eta_{\epsilon}(x),\psi_{\epsilon}(x)
and the speed

c_{\epsilon}

depend analytically w.r.t.
\mathtt{h}>0
.
Furthermore the following properties hold: for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, the Taylor coefficients
\eta_{\ell}(x),\psi_{\ell}(x)
are trigonometric polinomials

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\text{1. $\eta_{\ell}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}$
and $\psi_{\ell}(x)\in\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}$
according to Def. \ref{def:even}; }

(2.8)

\displaystyle\text{2. the coefficients
$\eta_{\ell}^{[0]}\,,\dots,\eta_{\ell}^{[\ell]}$,
${\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\psi_{\ell}^{[1]}\,,\dots{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\psi_{\ell}^{[\ell]}$
and
${\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}c_{\ell}$ belong to
${\mathcal{Q}}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})$, see Def. \ref{rationalstructure}.}

The
\eta_{\ell}^{[i]},\psi_{\ell}^{[i]}
are referred
as the Fourier-Taylor coefficients of the Stokes wave.

The proof of the structural properties (
2.8
) of the Stokes waves is given in Section

3
.

Proposition
3.14
will provide
further properties
of Stokes waves at
any
order of their Taylor expansion.
Specifically, we will demonstrate inductively the
second-order asymptotic expansion in the shallow-water limit

\mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}
of the Taylor-Fourier
coefficients
\eta_{\ell}^{[\ell]},\psi_{\ell}^{[\ell]}

valid for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
This latter result is crucial in proving that
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\to-\infty
as
\mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}
.

Remark 2.4
.

The local branches of Stokes wave solutions
of Theorem
2.3
have been
extended
to global branches encompassing extreme
waves, see e.g.
[
36
,
47
]
, through the application of analytic and topological bifurcation theory. More recently,
the existence of quasi-periodic traveling Stokes waves –which
represent the nonlinear superposition of periodic Stokes waves
with rationally independent speeds–
has also been demonstrated
in
[
4
,
24
,
5
]
using KAM methods.

Linearization at the Stokes waves.

In a reference frame moving with the speed

c_{\epsilon}
,
the linearized water waves equations
at the Stokes waves turn out to be
(after conjugating with the “good unknown of Alinhac” and the “Levi-Civita” transformations as in
[
42
,
6
,
7
,
9
]
)
the linear system

h_{t}={\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}h

(2.9)

where

{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}:H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2})

is the Hamiltonian and reversible real operator

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}:={\mathcal{L}}_{\epsilon}(\mathtt{h})

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{x}\circ({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))&|D|\tanh((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D|)\\
-(1+a_{\epsilon}(x))&({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))\partial_{x}\end{bmatrix}

(2.10)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}\begin{bmatrix}1+a_{\epsilon}(x)&-({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))\partial_{x}\\
\partial_{x}\circ({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))&|D|\tanh((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D|)\end{bmatrix}

where
\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon}
is a real number, analytic in
\epsilon
, and

p_{\epsilon}(x),a_{\epsilon}(x)
are real even analytic functions.
As a corollary of the properties
(
2.8
)
of the Stokes waves we prove in Section
3

the following
lemma.

Lemma 2.5
.

The functions
p_{\epsilon}(x)
,
a_{\epsilon}(x)
and the constant
\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon}
in (
2.10
) admit the Taylor expansions

\displaystyle p_{\epsilon}(x)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\epsilon^{\ell}p_{\ell}(x)\,,\quad a_{\epsilon}(x)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\epsilon^{\ell}a_{\ell}(x)\,,\quad\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq 2\,,\;\ell\textup{ even}\end{subarray}}\epsilon^{\ell}\mathtt{f}_{\ell}\,,

(2.11)

they are analytic with respect to
\mathtt{h}>0
and, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

1.

the functions
p_{\ell}(x)
,

a_{\ell}(x)
are trigonometric polynomials in

\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}
(see Definition
2.1
);

2.

the coefficients

{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}p_{\ell}^{[0]},\ldots,{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}p_{\ell}^{[\ell]}
,

a_{\ell}^{[0]},\ldots,a_{\ell}^{[\ell]}
and

\mathtt{f}_{\ell}
belong to

{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})
(see Definition
2.2
).

Bloch-Floquet analysis.

In view of (
1.3
)
we have to determine the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
-spectrum of the Floquet operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
. It turns out to be the complex
Hamiltonian
and
reversible
operator

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)\circ({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))&|D+\mu|\tanh\big((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D+\mu|\big)\\
-(1+a_{\epsilon}(x))&({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathrm{Id}\\
-\mathrm{Id}&0\end{bmatrix}}_{\displaystyle{=\mathcal{J}}}\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}1+a_{\epsilon}(x)&-({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)\\
(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)\circ({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}+p_{\epsilon}(x))&|D+\mu|\tanh\big((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D+\mu|\big)\end{bmatrix}}_{\displaystyle{=:{\mathcal{B}}(\mu,\epsilon)}={\mathcal{B}}(\mu,\epsilon)^{*}}\,,

(2.12)

that we regard as an operator with
domain
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}):=H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
and range
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}):=L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
,
equipped with
the complex scalar product

(f,g):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(f_{1}\overline{g_{1}}+f_{2}\overline{g_{2}}\right)\,\text{d}x\,,\quad\forall f=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}\\
f_{2}\end{bmatrix},\ \ g=\begin{bmatrix}g_{1}\\
g_{2}\end{bmatrix}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\,.

(2.13)

We also denote
\|f\|^{2}=(f,f)
. The
complex space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
is equipped
with the sesquilinear, skew-Hermitian and non-degenerate

complex symplectic form

{\mathcal{W}}_{c}\,\colon L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})\to\mathbb{C}\,,\quad{\mathcal{W}}_{c}(f,g):=(\mathcal{J}f,g)\,.

(2.14)

The complex operator

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}

is also reversible, namely

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}\circ\overline{\rho}=\overline{\rho}\circ{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}\,,\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad{\mathcal{B}}(\mu,\epsilon)\circ\overline{\rho}=-\overline{\rho}\circ{\mathcal{B}}(\mu,\epsilon)\,,

(2.15)

where
\overline{\rho}
is the complex involution (cfr. (
2.2
))

\overline{\rho}\begin{bmatrix}\eta(x)\\
\psi(x)\end{bmatrix}:=\begin{bmatrix}\overline{\eta}(-x)\\
-\overline{\psi}(-x)\end{bmatrix}\,.

(2.16)

Since the functions
\epsilon\mapsto a_{\epsilon}
,
p_{\epsilon}

are analytic as maps from
B(\epsilon_{0})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{T})

and
\epsilon\mapsto{\mathtt{f}}_{\epsilon}

is analytic as well,
the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
in (
2.12
) is analytic in
(\mu,\epsilon)
.

Remark 2.6
.

The operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}

is analytic also w.r. to
\mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{*}

for any
\mathtt{h}_{*}>0

provided
\epsilon
is small
depending on
\mathtt{h}_{*}
.
The key claim is that
the map

\mathtt{h}\mapsto\tanh(\mathtt{h}|D|)\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{s}_{0},H^{s}_{0})

is analytic, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since

\|\tanh(\mathtt{h}|D|)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(H^{s}_{0},H^{s}_{0})}=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}|\tanh(\mathtt{h}|j|)|

this amounts to prove the
analyticity of the map

\varphi:U_{\delta}\to\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})\,,\quad\mathtt{h}\mapsto\varphi(\mathtt{h}):=\big(\tanh(\mathtt{h}|j|)\big)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})\,,

in a complex neighborhood

U_{\delta}:=\{z=x+\mathrm{i}\,y\,,|x-\mathtt{h}_{0}|<\delta,|y|<\delta\}
of any
\mathtt{h}_{0}>0
.
By Theorem A.3 in
[
34
]

it is sufficient to show that

\varphi

is bounded
and each component

\tanh(\mathtt{h}|j|)

is analytic in
U_{\delta}
for some

\delta>0
small enough. This holds true because, for any

x>\mathtt{h}_{0}/2
we have

|\tanh(z|j|)|=\frac{|1-e^{-2(x+\mathrm{i}\,y)|j|}|}{|1+e^{-2(x+\mathrm{i}\,y)|j|}|}\leq\frac{1+e^{-2x|j|}}{1-e^{-2x|j|}}\leq\frac{2}{1-e^{-\mathtt{h}_{0}}}\,,\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

Our objective is to demonstrate the existence of eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}

with non zero real part.
Due to the Hamiltonian structure of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
, such eigenvalues can only emerge from multiple
eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}
, since if
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
then also
-\overline{\lambda}
is.
Consequently, simple purely imaginary eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}
persist on the imaginary axis under perturbation.

The spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}
.

The spectrum of the Fourier multiplier matrix operator

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)&|D+\mu|\tanh(\mathtt{h}|D+\mu|)\\
-1&{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}(\partial_{x}+\mathrm{i}\,\mu)\end{bmatrix}

(2.17)

on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
is given by the purely imaginary numbers

\lambda_{j}^{\sigma}(\mu,\mathtt{h}):=\mathrm{i}\,\omega^{\sigma}(j+\mu,\mathtt{h})=\mathrm{i}\,\Big({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}(j+\mu)-\sigma\Omega(j+\mu,\mathtt{h})\Big)\,,\ \forall\sigma=\pm\,,\,j\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(2.18)

where

\omega^{\sigma}(\varphi,\mathtt{h}):={\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\varphi-\sigma\Omega(\varphi,\mathtt{h})\,,\qquad\Omega(\varphi,\mathtt{h}):=\sqrt{\varphi\tanh(\mathtt{h}\varphi)}\,,\qquad\varphi\in\mathbb{R}\,,\ \ \sigma=\pm\,.

(2.19)

Note that
\omega^{+}(-\varphi,\mathtt{h})=-\omega^{-}(\varphi,\mathtt{h})
. Sometimes

\sigma
is called the Krein signature of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}^{\sigma}(\mu,\mathtt{h})
.

Restricted to
\varphi>0
, the function
\Omega(\cdot,\mathtt{h}):(0,+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)
is,
for any
\mathtt{h}>0
,
increasing and concave:

\displaystyle(\partial_{\varphi}\Omega)(\varphi,\mathtt{h})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\tanh(\mathtt{h}\varphi)+\mathtt{h}\varphi\big(1-\tanh^{2}(\mathtt{h}\varphi)\big)}{2\sqrt{\varphi\tanh(\mathtt{h}\varphi)}}>0\,,

(2.20)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\varphi\varphi}\Omega)(\varphi,\mathtt{h})

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{\tanh^{2}(\mathtt{h}\varphi)\big[4\mathtt{h}^{2}\varphi^{2}\big(1-\tanh^{2}(\mathtt{h}\varphi)\big)+\big(1-\mathtt{h}\varphi\frac{1-\tanh^{2}(\mathtt{h}\varphi)}{\tanh(\mathtt{h}\varphi)}\big)^{2}\big]}{4(\varphi\tanh(\mathtt{h}\varphi))^{3/2}}<0\,.

For any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
such that
j+\mu\neq 0

we have
\Omega(j+\mu,\mathtt{h})\neq 0
and we associate
to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}^{\sigma}(\mu,\mathtt{h})
the eigenvector

\displaystyle f^{\sigma}_{j}:=f^{\sigma}_{j}(\mu,\mathtt{h}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\Omega(j+\mu,\mathtt{h})}}\begin{bmatrix}-\sqrt{\sigma}\,\Omega(j+\mu,\mathtt{h})\\
\sqrt{-\sigma}\end{bmatrix}e^{\mathrm{i}\,jx}\,,

(2.21)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}f_{j}^{\sigma}(\mu,\mathtt{h})=\lambda_{j}^{\sigma}(\mu,\mathtt{h})f_{j}^{\sigma}(\mu,\mathtt{h})\,,

which satisfy, recalling (
2.16
),
the reversibility property

\overline{\rho}f_{j}^{+}(\mu,\mathtt{h})=f_{j}^{+}(\mu,\mathtt{h})\,,\quad\overline{\rho}f_{j}^{-}(\mu,\mathtt{h})=-f_{j}^{-}(\mu,\mathtt{h})\,.

(2.22)

For any
\mu\notin\mathbb{Z}
we have
\Omega(j+\mu,\mathtt{h})\neq 0
for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}

and the family of eigenvectors
\{f_{j}^{\sigma}(\mu)\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
in (
2.21
) forms a
complex symplectic basis
of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
,
with respect to the complex symplectic form
{\mathcal{W}}_{c}
in (
2.14
), namely its elements are linearly independent, span densely
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
and satisfy,
for any pairs of integers
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and any pair of signs
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\{\pm\}
,

{\mathcal{W}}_{c}\big(f_{j}^{\sigma},f_{j^{\prime}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\big)=\begin{cases}-\mathrm{i}\,&\text{if }j=j^{\prime}\text{ and }\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}=+\,,\\
\ \;\mathrm{i}\,&\text{if }j=j^{\prime}\text{ and }\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}=-\,,\\
\ \,0&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(2.23)

The choice of the normalization constant in (
2.21
)
implies (
2.23
).

To describe the multiple eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}
we need the following result
proved in Appendix
A
.

Lemma 2.7

(
Spectral collisions).

For any depth
\mathtt{h}>0
and any integer
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
, the following holds.

i)

For any
\sigma\in\{\pm\}

the equations

\omega^{\sigma}\big(\varphi,\mathtt{h}\big)=\omega^{\sigma}\big(\varphi+\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}\big)

have no solutions, see Figure
4
.

ii)

The equation

\omega^{-}\big(\varphi,\mathtt{h}\big)=\omega^{+}\big(\varphi+\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}\big)

has a unique positive solution
\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})>0
,

\omega^{-}\big(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}),\mathtt{h}\big)=\omega^{+}\big(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})+\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}\big)={\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})+\Omega(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}))=:\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})>0\,,

(2.24)

see Figure
5
,
and the negative solution
-\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})-\mathtt{p}
,

\omega^{-}\big(-\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})-\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}\big)=\omega^{+}\big(-\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}),\mathtt{h}\big)=-\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})<0\,,

(2.25)

which is unique for any
\mathtt{p}\geq 3
. For
\mathtt{p}=2
the equation

\omega^{-}\big(\varphi,\mathtt{h}\big)=\omega^{+}\big(\varphi+2,\mathtt{h}\big)

possesses also
the negative solution
\varphi=-1
and

\omega^{-}\big(-1,\mathtt{h}\big)=\omega^{+}\big(1,\mathtt{h}\big)=0
.

iii)

The equation
\omega^{+}(\varphi,\mathtt{h})=\omega^{-}(\varphi+\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})
has no solutions.

The positive solution
\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})
of (
2.24
)
satisfies the following properties:

1.

The function
\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\cdot):(0,+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)
,

\mathtt{h}\mapsto\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})
, is analytic.

2.

For any
\mathtt{h}>0
the sequence
\mathtt{p}\mapsto\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})
is increasing

0<\underline{\varphi}(2,\mathtt{h})<\ldots<\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})<\ldots\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{\mathtt{p}\to+\infty}\,\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})=+\infty\,.

(2.26)

3.

For any
\mathtt{h}>0
it results

\begin{cases}\underline{\varphi}(2,\mathtt{h})\in(0,1)\\
\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})\in\big(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p}-1,\mathtt{h}),\frac{(\mathtt{p}-1)^{2}}{4}\big)\,,\ \forall\mathtt{p}\geq 3\,.\end{cases}

Furthermore there is
\mathtt{h}_{*}>0
such that

\underline{\varphi}(2,\mathtt{h})>1/4
for any
\mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{*}
.

4.

It results

\lim_{\mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}}\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})=0\,,\qquad\lim_{\mathtt{h}\to+\infty}\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})=\tfrac{1}{4}(\mathtt{p}-1)^{2}\,,

(2.27)

more precisely

\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})=\tfrac{1}{12}\mathtt{p}(\mathtt{p}^{2}-1)\mathtt{h}^{2}+\frac{19-15\mathtt{p}^{2}-4\mathtt{p}^{4}}{720}\mathtt{p}\mathtt{h}^{4}+O(\mathtt{h}^{6})\qquad\text{as}\ \mathtt{h}\to 0^{+}\,,

(2.28)

and

\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})=\frac{(\mathtt{p}-1)^{2}}{4}+\begin{cases}\frac{3}{8}e^{-\frac{\mathtt{h}}{2}}+o\big(e^{-\frac{\mathtt{h}}{2}}\big)&\text{if}\ \mathtt{p}=2\,,\\
-\frac{8}{3}e^{-2\mathtt{h}}+o\big(e^{-2\mathtt{h}}\big)&\text{if}\ \mathtt{p}=3\,,\\
-\frac{\mathtt{p}^{2}-1}{2}e^{-2\mathtt{h}}+o\big(e^{-2\mathtt{h}}\big)&\;\forall\,\mathtt{p}\geq 4\,,\end{cases}\qquad\text{as}\ \mathtt{h}\to+\infty\,.

(2.29)

Remark 2.8
.

For
\mathtt{p}=0,1
, the equations

\omega^{\sigma}\big(\varphi,\mathtt{h}\big)=\omega^{\sigma^{\prime}}\big(\varphi+\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}\big)

admit other solutions
all
at the common
value
0
(follow the proof of Lemma
2.7
).
This gives rise to the quadruple zero eigenvalue of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}

from which the Benjamin-Feir instability originates, analyzed in
[
6
,
8
,
9
]
.

As a corollary of Lemma
2.7
we deduce
in Appendix
A

the complete description of
all

the multiple
nonzero
eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}
.

Lemma 2.9

(
Multiple eigenvalues of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}
away from
0
).

For any depth
\mathtt{h}>0
and any
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, the spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}
away from zero contains only simple or double eigenvalues: for
any integer
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
,

1.

for any

\mu\neq\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})
,
mod
1, all the eigenvalues of
the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}
in (
2.17
) are simple;

2.

for any

\mu=\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})
,
mod

1
,
the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}
has the non-zero double eigenvalue

\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})=\mathrm{i}\,\omega^{-}\big(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}),\mathtt{h}\big)=\mathrm{i}\,\omega^{+}\big(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})+\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}\big)\,,

(2.30)

and
the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{-\mu,0}
has the complex conjugated non-zero double eigenvalue

-\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})=\mathrm{i}\,\omega^{-}\big(-\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})-\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}\big)=\mathrm{i}\,\omega^{+}\big(-\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}),\mathtt{h}\big)\,.

The sequence
\mathtt{p}\mapsto\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
is increasing

0<\omega_{*}^{(2)}(\mathtt{h})<\ldots<\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})<\ldots\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{\mathtt{p}\to+\infty}\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})=+\infty\,.

(2.31)

The eigenspace of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,0}
associated with the
double eigenvalue
\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
in (
2.30
) is spanned
by the two
eigenvectors

\displaystyle f_{k}^{-}(\mu,\mathtt{h})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\Omega(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}),\mathtt{h})}}\begin{bmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\,\,\Omega(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}),\mathtt{h})\\
1\end{bmatrix}e^{\mathrm{i}\,kx}\,,

(2.32)

\displaystyle f_{k^{\prime}}^{+}(\mu,\mathtt{h})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\Omega(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})+\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})}}\begin{bmatrix}-\Omega(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})+\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})\\
\mathrm{i}\,\end{bmatrix}e^{\mathrm{i}\,k^{\prime}x}\,,

where
k:=\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})-\mu\in\mathbb{Z}
and
k^{\prime}:=k+\mathtt{p}
.

(a)

*

[.4]

(b)

*

[.4]

Figure 4
:
Absence of collisions for eigenvalues with same Krein signature for any
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
.

Figure 5
:
Collision of eigenvalues with opposite Krein signature for

\mathtt{p}\geq 2
.

In view of Lemma
2.9
,
given an integer
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
, we fix

\underline{\mu}:=\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})\,,\quad k:=0\,,\quad k^{\prime}:=\mathtt{p}\,,\quad\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h}):=\omega^{-}(\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}),\mathtt{h})=\omega^{+}(\mathtt{p}+\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h}),\mathtt{h})\,.

(2.33)

Remark 2.10
.

We will consider depths
\mathtt{h}>0

such that
\underline{\mu}=\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})\notin\mathbb{Z}
,
so that
the family of eigenvectors

\{f_{j}^{\sigma}(\underline{\mu})\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
in
(
2.21
) form a
complex symplectic basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{2})
.
This is convenient for the computations in Section

5.4
, as we outline at the beginning of that section, without introducing any restrictions.

The spectrum of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{\mu},0}
decomposes into two disjoint parts

\sigma({\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{\mu},0})=\sigma_{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{\mu},0})\cup\sigma_{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime\prime}({\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{\mu},0})\qquad\text{where }\quad\sigma_{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{\mu},0}):=\big\{\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\big\}\,.\quad

(2.34)

By Kato’s perturbation theory (see Lemma
4.1
below)
for any
(\mu,\epsilon)
sufficiently close to
(\underline{\mu},0)
, the perturbed spectrum

\sigma\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)
still
admits a disjoint decomposition

\sigma\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)=\sigma_{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)\cup\sigma_{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime\prime}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)\,,\quad\forall\mathtt{p}\geq 2\,,

where
\sigma_{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)
consists of 2 eigenvalues close to the double eigenvalue
\mathrm{i}\,\omega_{*}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})

of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\underline{\mu},0}
.
We denote by
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mu,\epsilon}^{(\mathtt{p})}
the spectral subspace associated to
\sigma_{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}\right)
, which has dimension 2 and it is invariant under
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
.

Theorem 2.11
.

For any integer
\mathtt{p}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathtt{p}\geq 2
, for any depth
\mathtt{h}>0
,
there exist
\epsilon_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}>0
,
\delta_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}>0
such that
the operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mu,\epsilon}\colon{\mathcal{V}}_{\mu,\epsilon}\to{\mathcal{V}}_{\mu,\epsilon}
, defined for any
(\mu,\epsilon)=(\underline{\mu}+\delta,\epsilon)\in B_{\delta_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}}(\underline{\mu})\times B_{\epsilon_{0}^{(\mathtt{p})}}(0)
with

\underline{\mu}:=\underline{\varphi}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{h})>0
in (
2.33
), can be represented
by a
2\times 2

matrix with identical real off-diagonal entries and purely imaginary diagonal entries of the form

\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{i}\,\omega^{+}(\underline{\mu}+\mathtt{p}+\delta,\mathtt{h})+\mathrm{i}\,r_{1}(\epsilon^{2})&\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\,\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}}+r_{2}(\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}+2},\delta\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}})\\[5.69054pt]
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})\,\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}}+r_{2}(\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}+2},\delta\epsilon^{\mathtt{p}})&\mathrm{i}\,\omega^{-}(\underline{\mu}+\delta,\mathtt{h})+\mathrm{i}\,r_{3}(\epsilon^{2})\end{pmatrix}\,,

where
\omega^{\pm}(\varphi,\mathtt{h})
is given in (
2.19
) and
\beta_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
is a real analytic function satisfying
(
1.5
).

Theorem
2.11
implies Theorem
1.2
, by using Theorem
4.7
.
The form (
1.9
) of the approximated ellipse follows by
Lemma

4.8

with

E^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h}):=\frac{\gamma_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})-\alpha_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})}{\gamma_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})+\alpha_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})}

and

\alpha_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h}),\gamma_{1}^{(\mathtt{p})}(\mathtt{h})
in (
5.14
)-(
5.15
).

3
Expansion of the Stokes wave at any order

Section
3.1
presents the proofs for the structural properties (
2.8
) of Stokes waves, as stated in
Theorem
2.3

and Lemma
2.5
.
Furthermore, we prove Propositions
3.14
and

3.16
, which offer key information about the Stokes wave’s Fourier-Taylor expansion at any order in both shallow and deep water.

3.1
Structure of the Stokes wave

We demonstrate that the Taylor expansion of the Stokes wave maintains the structure outlined in (
2.8
) at every order.
This is achieved by iteratively constructing the solution of system (
2.6
).
It is advantageous to introduce formal power series of trigonometric polynomials.

Definition 3.1
.

We denote
\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
, respectively
\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
, the module over the ring
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})
(introduced in Definition
2.2
) of formal power series

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}(x)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\epsilon^{\ell}f_{\ell}(x)\quad\text{with}\quad f_{\ell}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}\quad\text{with coefficients }\quad f_{\ell}^{[0]},\dots,f_{\ell}^{[\ell]}\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}),

(3.1)

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(x)=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\epsilon^{\ell}g_{\ell}(x)\quad\text{with}\quad g_{\ell}(x)\in\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}\quad\text{with coefficients }\quad g_{\ell}^{[1]},\dots,g_{\ell}^{[\ell]}\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})\,,

where

\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}

and
\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}

are the spaces of trigonometric polynomials of degree
\ell
introduced
in Definition
2.1
.
We denote by
\mathbb{R}\cap\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
the one-dimensional submodule of constants in

\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
.
Given a function
f_{\epsilon}(x)
we denote
[f_{\epsilon}(x)]_{\ell}:=f_{\ell}(x)

its Taylor coefficient of order

\ell
in (
3.1
) and, for any

\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
we denote

f_{\ell}^{[\kappa]}:=[f_{\epsilon}(x)]_{\ell}^{[\kappa]}=[f_{\ell}(x)]^{[\kappa]}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f_{\ell}(x)\cos(\kappa x)\,dx\quad\text{if}\ \ell\ \text{is even,}\\
\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f_{\ell}(x)\sin(\kappa x)\,dx\quad\text{if}\ \ell\ \text{is odd,}\end{cases}\quad f_{\ell}^{[0]}:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f_{\ell}(x)\mathrm{d}x\,,

the
\kappa
-harmonic coefficient
of
f_{\ell}(x)
.

We shall also consider the “shifted” modules over
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})
given by
{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
and
{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
. Note that
{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)=\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
and
{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2}\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)=\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
.

We now list some properties of the formal power series in

\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
and
\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
.
Similar results hold, mutatis mutandis, by replacing
\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
with
{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
or
\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
with
{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
.

The first result follows directly from Definitions
2.1
-
2.2
and
3.1
.

Lemma 3.2

(
Product).

For any
f_{\epsilon}(x),\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
and
g_{\epsilon}(x),\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
, one has

f_{\epsilon}(x)\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}(x)\,,\;g_{\epsilon}(x)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\,,\qquad f_{\epsilon}(x)\tilde{g}_{\epsilon}(x)\,,\;g_{\epsilon}(x)\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\,.

(3.2)

For any
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and
f_{\ell_{1}}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell_{1}}
,
f_{\ell_{2}}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell_{2}}
,
g_{\ell_{1}}(x)\in\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell_{1}}
and
g_{\ell_{2}}(x)\in\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell_{2}}
one has

\displaystyle f_{\ell_{1}}(x)f_{\ell_{2}}(x)\,,\;g_{\ell_{1}}(x)g_{\ell_{2}}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\,,\qquad f_{\ell_{1}}(x)g_{\ell_{2}}(x)\,,\;g_{\ell_{1}}(x)f_{\ell_{2}}(x)\in\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}\,.

(3.3)

We now consider the action of
Fourier multipliers.

Lemma 3.3
.

(Fourier multipliers)

The differential operator
\partial_{x}
,
the flat-sea Dirichlet-Neumann operator

G_{0}=|D|\tanh{(\mathtt{h}|D|)}
, the
Hilbert transform

\mathcal{H}:=-\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{sgn}(D)
and
|D|
satisfy, for any

\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\begin{aligned} &\partial_{x}:\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}\to\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}\,,\ \ \partial_{x}:\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}\to\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}\,,\\
&G_{0}:\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}\to\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}\,,\ G_{0}:\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}\to\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}\,,\\
&\mathcal{H}:\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}\to\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}\,,\ \ \mathcal{H}:\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}\to\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}\,,\\
&|D|:\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}\to\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}\,,\ |D|:\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}\to\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}\,,\end{aligned}\quad\ \begin{aligned} &\partial_{x}:\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\to\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\,,\ \ \partial_{x}:\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\to\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\,,\\
&G_{0}:\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\to\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\,,\ G_{0}:\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\to\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\,,\\
&\mathcal{H}:\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\to\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\,,\ \ \mathcal{H}:\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\to\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\,,\\
&|D|:\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\to\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\,,\ |D|:\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\to\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\,\,.\end{aligned}

(3.4)

Proof.

We prove the statement for
G_{0}
. Since
the symbol

|\xi|\tanh(\mathtt{h}|\xi|)
is real and even
in
\xi
we have that

G_{0}
preserves the parity and,
for any
f_{\epsilon}(x)
in
\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
or
\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
, for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

[G_{0}f_{\epsilon}]_{\ell}^{[\kappa]}=\kappa\tanh(\mathtt{h}\kappa)f_{\ell}^{[\kappa]}\,,\quad\tanh(\mathtt{h}\kappa)=\Big(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\,\textup{odd}\end{subarray}}^{\kappa}{\kappa\choose j}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2j}\Big)\,\Big/\,\Big(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\,\textup{even}\end{subarray}}^{\kappa}{\kappa\choose j}{\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2j}\Big)\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})\,,

(3.5)

where the latter formula comes by induction using

\tanh(a+b)=\frac{\tanh(a)+\tanh(b)}{1+\tanh(a)\tanh(b)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{k\choose j}+{k\choose j-1}={k+1\choose j}\,.

Property (
3.4
)
for
\partial_{x}
,
\mathcal{H}

and
|D|

is similar.
∎

A key property used repeatedly hereafter is that the leading Fourier coefficients of a product of functions within

\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
or
\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)

depend solely on the leading Fourier coefficients of each factor, according to the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
it results

\big[f_{\epsilon}(x)g_{\epsilon}(x)\big]_{\ell}^{[\ell]}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell}f_{\ell_{1}}^{[\ell_{1}]}g_{\ell_{2}}^{[\ell_{2}]}\qquad\text{if}\ f_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\,,\ g_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\,\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\text{or}\ f_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\,,\ g_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\,,\\
-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell}f_{\ell_{1}}^{[\ell_{1}]}g_{\ell_{2}}^{[\ell_{2}]}\,\quad\text{if}\ f_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\,,\ g_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\,.\end{cases}

Proof.

We make the proof for

f_{\epsilon}(x),g_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
. For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\big[f_{\epsilon}(x)g_{\epsilon}(x)\big]_{\ell}^{[\ell]}=\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell,\atop j_{1}\leq\ell_{1},j_{2}\leq\ell_{2}}\big[f_{\ell_{1}}^{[j_{1}]}g_{\ell_{2}}^{[j_{2}]}\cos(j_{1}x)\cos(j_{2}x)\big]^{[\ell]}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell}f_{\ell_{1}}^{[\ell_{1}]}g_{\ell_{2}}^{[\ell_{2}]}

because
\cos((j_{1}+j_{2})x)=\cos(\ell x)

just for
j_{1}=\ell_{1}

and
j_{2}=\ell_{2}
. The other cases are similar.
∎

We now consider the composition of
functions belonging to the spaces in Definition
3.1
.

Lemma 3.5

(
Composition).

Let
f_{\epsilon}(x)
be a function in
\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
and
g_{\epsilon}(x)
in
\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
.
Then the composite
function

f_{\epsilon}\big(x+g_{\epsilon}(x)\big)
belongs to

\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
.
In addition, if
\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
satisfies

\tilde{f}_{\ell}^{[\ell]}=f_{\ell}^{[\ell]}

for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\big[f_{\epsilon}(x+g_{\epsilon}(x))\big]_{\ell}^{[\ell]}=\big[\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}(x+g_{\epsilon}(x))\big]_{\ell}^{[\ell]}\,,\quad\forall\ell\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(3.6)

Proof.

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\big[f_{\epsilon}\big(x+g_{\epsilon}(x)\big)\big]_{\ell}=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\sum_{\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{k}=\ell\atop\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}\geq 1}\frac{1}{(k-1)!}(\partial_{x}^{k-1}f_{\ell_{1}})(x)g_{\ell_{2}}(x)\ldots g_{\ell_{k}}(x)

(3.7)

where we set
\partial_{x}^{0}:=\text{Id}
. By Lemmata
3.2
and

3.3

we deduce that
\big[f_{\epsilon}\big(x+g_{\epsilon}(x)\big)\big]_{\ell}
belongs to
\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}
. In view of the product structure (
3.7
) and using Lemma
3.4
,
the leading harmonic

\big[f_{\epsilon}\big(x+g_{\epsilon}(x)\big)\big]_{\ell}^{[\ell]}
depends on

f_{\epsilon}(x)
only through the
leading coefficients

f_{\ell_{1}}^{[\ell_{1}]}
.
This proves (
3.6
).
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
f_{\epsilon}(x),\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
and
g_{\epsilon}(x)\in\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
.
Then
\dfrac{f_{\epsilon}}{1+\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}}\in\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
and
\dfrac{g_{\epsilon}}{1+\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}}\in\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
.

Proof.

We have

\dfrac{f_{\epsilon}}{1+\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}}=f_{\epsilon}+\sum\limits_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{k}f_{\epsilon}\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}^{k}=\sum\limits_{\ell\geq 1}\epsilon^{\ell}\Big(f_{\ell}(x)+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell-1}(-1)^{k}\sum\limits_{\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{k+1}=\ell}f_{\ell_{1}}(x)\tilde{f}_{\ell_{2}}(x)\dots\tilde{f}_{\ell_{k+1}}(x)\Big)

and we deduce that
\Big[\dfrac{f_{\epsilon}}{1+\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}}\Big]_{\ell}
belongs to
\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell}
by (
3.3
). Similarly one proves that
\Big[\dfrac{g_{\epsilon}}{1+\tilde{f}_{\epsilon}}\Big]_{\ell}\in\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell}
.
∎

We now consider the Dirichlet-Neumann operator
G(\eta)

that can be represented as

G(\eta)=G_{0}+\sum_{\ell\geq 1}G_{\ell}(\eta)\quad\text{where}\quad G_{0}=|D|\tanh{(\mathtt{h}|D|)}\,.

Lemma 3.7

(
Dirichlet-Neumann).

Let
\eta_{\epsilon}\in\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
.
The Dirichlet-Neumann operator

G(\eta_{\epsilon})
and its Taylor components

G_{\ell}(\eta_{\epsilon})

satisfy

G(\eta_{\epsilon})\,,G_{\ell}(\eta_{\epsilon})\,:\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\to\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\,\,,\qquad\forall\ell\in\mathbb{N}\,.

Proof.

The Taylor coefficient

G_{\ell}(\eta)

are recursively given by
[
16
, formulae (39)-(40)]

\displaystyle G_{2r}(\eta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(2r)!}G_{0}|D|^{2r-2}\partial_{x}\eta^{2r}\partial_{x}-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\frac{1}{(2r-2s)!}|D|^{2r-2s}\eta^{2r-2s}G_{2s}(\eta)

(3.8)

\displaystyle-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\frac{1}{(2r-2s-1)!}G_{0}|D|^{2r-2s-2}\eta^{2r-2s-1}G_{2s+1}(\eta)\,,

\displaystyle G_{2r-1}(\eta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(2r-1)!}|D|^{2r-2}\partial_{x}\eta^{2r-1}\partial_{x}-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\frac{1}{(2r-2s-1)!}G_{0}|D|^{2r-2s-2}\eta^{2r-2s-1}G_{2s}(\eta)

\displaystyle-\sum_{s=0}^{r-2}\frac{1}{(2r-2s-2)!}|D|^{2r-2s-2}\eta^{2r-2s-2}G_{2s+1}(\eta)\,.

By induction and exploiting Lemmata
3.2
-
3.3
one shows that
G_{\ell}(\eta_{\epsilon}):\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)\to\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)
.
∎

We also use the following lemma.
We remind that
\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}\cap\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)
.

Lemma 3.8
.

\mathcal{H}\coth((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D|):\mathtt{Evn}(\epsilon)\to\mathtt{Odd}(\epsilon)

as well as

[\mathcal{H}\coth((\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon})|D|)]_{\ell}
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Let
g(z):=\coth(z)
.
By (
3.7
) we have

\big[g\big(\mathtt{h}|D|+\mathtt{f}_{\epsilon}|D|\big)\eta_{\epsilon}\big]_{\ell}=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\sum_{\ell_{1}+\dots+\ell_{k}=\ell}\frac{1}{(k-1)!}|D|^{k-1}(\partial_{z}^{k-1}g)(\mathtt{h}|D|)\eta_{\ell_{1}}(x){\mathtt{f}}_{\ell_{2}}\ldots{\mathtt{f}}_{\ell_{k}}\,.

(3.9)

It results that

(\partial_{z}^{k-1}g)(\mathtt{h}|j|)
belong to

{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})
.
This follows by (
3.5
) for

k=1
and inductively for the higher order derivatives.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.3
.
The existence of a
solution of (
2.6
)
follows as in
[
7
]

by the Crandall-Rabinowitz
bifurcation theorem from a simple eigenvalue. The fact that
\epsilon_{*}>0
is uniform with respect to
\mathtt{h}>\mathtt{h}_{*}
follows by Remark
3.11
.
We now prove the structural properties (
2.8
).

We are going to prove inductively the following statements
about the coefficients of the Stokes waves in (
2.7
):
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}

(S\eta)_{\ell}

the functions
\eta_{\ell^{\prime}}(x)
,
\ell^{\prime}=1,\ldots,\ell
,
belong to

\mathtt{Evn}_{\ell^{\prime}}
with coefficients

\eta_{\ell^{\prime}}^{[0]}\,,\ldots,\eta_{\ell^{\prime}}^{[\ell^{\prime}]}\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathtt{c}}_{\mathtt{h}}^{2})
;

(S\psi)_{\ell}

the functions
\psi_{\ell^{\prime}}(x)
,
\ell^{\prime}=1,\ldots,\ell
,
belong to

\mathtt{Odd}_{\ell^{\prime}}
with coefficients
